We couldn't leave the house for three days. The power went out, so we had to huddle around the fireplace. My wife made a big pot of chili right before the stove died, thank goodness. We ate cold chili for two days straight. You'd think it would be awful, but it actually wasn't bad. The beans kind of soaked up all the spices... \n\nBut anyway, no, I'm not feeling sad or low, if that's what you mean. Just... not feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't think so. My mind is still pretty sharp, thankfully. I still do the crossword puzzle in the morning paper without much trouble. Well, I try to, anyway. They changed the font on the crossword a few months back, made it so tiny you practically need a microscope just to read the clues across and down. I actually called the editor of the paper to complain about it. Left a message with some secretary. Never got a call back, of course. \n\nPeople just don't have the decency to return a phone call anymore. Everything is a text message or an email now. My grandson, Tommy, he sent me a text message for my birthday last year. Just a little cartoon picture of a cake on the phone screen. I mean, you can't put a text message on the mantlepiece, can you? Used to be people sent real cards. You'd go to the pharmacy and pick out a nice Hallmark. \n\nBut, uh... no, my thinking is fine. I can concentrate just fine if I want to. I just don't have much to concentrate on these days besides the clock ticking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, didn't you just ask me that a minute ago? Or maybe it was a different question. My memory's fine, but sometimes all these questions blur together. Like I said, I wouldn't call it feeling down or depressed. I just feel kind of numb, you know? Blank. \n\nIt's sort of like how the TV screen used to look when a channel went off the air late at night. You remember that? The static? Just a bunch of black and white fuzz and that loud hissing noise. We used to have this old Zenith console television set. Weighed a ton. Had the record player built right into the top of it. I remember the day we brought it home. The delivery guys had to wedge it through the front door, scratched up the doorframe pretty good. My wife was furious. She chased them out with a broom! Oh, she had a temper, she did. But she loved that television. We used to sit and watch Ed Sullivan on it every Sunday night...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I don't feel depressed. Just... like that static on the old TV. Just a whole lot of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped finding pleasure or interest in any of the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like sitting out on the porch with my coffee and watching the blue jays. I put out peanuts for them. You have to use the unsalted kind in the shell, otherwise they won't even look at them. \n\nI learned that from my old neighbor, Frank. Frank was a real character. He had this old Ford pickup truck, a '65 I think it was, cherry red. He used to spend every single Saturday out in his driveway just waxing that thing. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps, I swear. He ended up selling it a few years back to some kid from two towns over. Made a pretty penny on it, too, but I told him he was crazy to let it go. They just don't make engines like they used to, you know? Everything is run by computers now. You open the hood of a car today and it looks like a spaceship in there. You can't even change your own spark plugs without a degree in electronics... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. No, I still like my birds and my crosswords just fine. I haven't lost interest in them or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt more tired or noticed a lack of energy lately, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm any more tired than usual. I mean, I'm 79, I'm not running marathons, but I have enough energy to get through the day. I still get up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I did when I worked at the plant. \n\nThirty-five years I worked at that manufacturing plant down by the river. We made parts for washing machines, mostly. The noise in that place, let me tell you, it was something else. Clanking and banging all day long. You had to wear these heavy ear muffs, and even then, your ears would ring for hours after your shift. I worked with a fella named Mickey. Mickey was missing half his left index finger. Lost it to a stamping press back in '78. He used to joke that it made him better at bowling, said it gave the ball a special spin. He was a funny guy, Mickey. He used to bring these elaborate sandwiches for lunch, like pastrami with coleslaw right on the bread. His wife made them. She was a lovely woman, made the best potato salad for the company picnic every year...\n\nBut, uh, energy. Yeah. No, my energy is fine. I'm not dragging myself around or anything like that. Just my normal self.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with a loss of interest in some activities and feeling empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in everything, like I told you. But this... this empty feeling, the static... it's been about a week. Ever since that robbery outside the bank. \n\nI was just going to cash my pension check, you know? They still mail the paper check. My daughter keeps telling me to set up the direct deposit on the computer, but I don't trust those things. Hackers and whatnot. You hear about it on the news all the time, people waking up and their whole life savings is just gone, poof, into thin air. I prefer to have the paper in my hand. Anyway, I always go to the same teller, Margaret. She's been there for at least fifteen years. Always asks about my granddaughter. Margaret has this little ceramic cat on her counter, right next to the pen on the chain. She said her nephew made it for her in art class... \n\nBut, yes, about a week now. Since the... the incident at the bank. Just feeling blank since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Down? Low mood? No, I wouldn't say that. Like I said, it's more of a... a blankness. A static. But not sad. I mean, I haven't been crying or moping around the house. I still keep the place tidy. You have to keep a tidy house, my late wife always said that. A cluttered house is a cluttered mind, she used to say. \n\nShe was a stickler for dusting, my Martha. Every Tuesday and Friday, out came the lemon Pledge and that yellow microfiber cloth. She wouldn't let anyone else do it, either. Said I always missed the top of the doorframes. We had this beautiful mahogany dining table, inherited it from her grandmother. Heavy thing, took four men to move it when we bought the house. She'd polish that table until it shined like a mirror. We used to host Thanksgiving every year, twenty people crammed into that dining room. The turkey always took twice as long to cook as the recipe said...\n\nBut no, I'm not down or depressed. Just... empty, I guess. And it's only been a week, not two. Ever since the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not interested in almost anything lately, even things that used to matter a lot to you?\n\nPatient: Not interested in anything? No, I wouldn't go that far. I still care about things. I still read the morning paper, front to back, every single day. The delivery boy, Tommy, he always throws it right onto the azalea bushes. I've asked him a hundred times to leave it on the porch step, but kids these days, they're always in such a rush. He rides this bright green bicycle, one of those mountain bikes with the thick tires. Reminds me of the Schwinn I bought for my son back in '85. Beautiful bike, cherry red. He rode that thing everywhere until he got his driver's license. First car he bought was a beat-up old Ford Taurus, mustard yellow. Ugliest car you've ever seen, but he loved it. Spent every weekend under the hood, trying to fix the radiator... \n\nBut anyway, no, I still care about things. I still do my crossword puzzles. It's just that... well, things just feel a bit muffled right now, if that makes sense. But I haven't lost interest in what matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Didn't I just... I thought I just answered that, Doc. No, I haven't had days like that. Like I said, it's not a low mood or being sad, just that blankness. Are you sure you're writing this down? You sound a bit like my neighbor, Arthur. He's always asking me the exact same questions twice. 'Did you get your gutters cleaned out?' 'Did you see the weather report?' Every single time I see him at the fence. \n\nHe's got this old golden retriever, Buster. Sweet dog, but completely deaf now. Arthur will stand on the back porch hollering and hollering for him, and Buster is just fast asleep under the hydrangea bushes, oblivious to the whole world. Beautiful hydrangeas, though. His wife planted those before she passed away. They turn the most vibrant shade of blue if you put coffee grounds in the soil. Martha and I tried that with our rose bushes once, but it just attracted ants. Thousands of them, marching right up the side of the brickwork... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, no. No days where I'm just sitting around sad all the time. Just the static since I was at the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two weeks in a row where you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you pulling my leg? Because I could swear you just asked me that exact same thing. Two weeks in a row, down or low mood... no, I haven't. I really haven't. It's only been a week since the whole thing at the bank anyway. \n\nYou know, this reminds me of the time I had to get my driver's license renewed down at the DMV. The clerk there—a young gal with these enormous pink fingernails, I don't know how she could even type—she asked me for my address three different times. Three times! I finally said to her, 'Miss, if I move while I'm standing in this line, I'll be sure to let you know.' The fellow behind me got a real kick out of that. He was wearing this old navy blue windbreaker, had a patch from the USS Nimitz on the shoulder. My brother served in the Navy, you know. Stationed out in San Diego back in the sixties. He used to send these postcards with the palm trees and the ocean... beautiful handwriting, cursive, the kind they don't teach in schools anymore. \n\nBut no, Doc, to answer your question again... no two weeks of being down or sad. Just the muffler feeling. The blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where most of the day, nearly every day, you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we... is there an echo in here? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. Hopeless, depressed, two weeks... I just told you, it hasn't even been two weeks since the bank robbery. It's been a week. So no, I haven't had two weeks of anything. \n\nThis is starting to feel like that movie, what was it called... Groundhog Day. With Bill Murray. Martha loved that movie. We must have rented it from Blockbuster a dozen times. Remember Blockbuster? You'd go in on a Friday night, the smell of popcorn right when you walked in the door, the blue and yellow carpet... They had this whole wall just for new releases, but you had to get there early or all the good ones were gone. I always ended up picking something from the discount bin. Found a lot of John Wayne classics that way, though. 'The Searchers,' now that's a picture. They shot it in Monument Valley. The colors of those rocks... red and orange against the blue sky. I always wanted to take an RV trip out there, drive Route 66 all the way to the coast...\n\nBut honestly, Doc, if you ask me if I've been sad for two weeks one more time, I might just have to check your hearing aid. No, no two weeks of being depressed. Just... just the blank feeling since the bank. That's all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. Two weeks? Again with the two weeks. I keep telling you, it's only been a single week since the whole thing at the bank. So no, I haven't had two weeks of losing interest in anything, because the math just doesn't add up. 

Besides, I still like my hobbies. I've got an O-gauge model train setup down in the basement that takes up half the room. Started building it back in '82. Martha used to complain about the dust, but she'd come down and help me paint the little figurines anyway. We had this tiny little station master... she painted his hat a bright crimson. Reminds me of the conductor on the old Santa Fe line I took with my father when I was just a boy. The seats were covered in this terribly scratchy wool, and the smell of the diesel... boy, you don't ever forget a smell like that. My father bought me a ham sandwich in the dining car. Cost him a nickel, I think. Or maybe a dime. Things were so cheap back then. You know, yesterday I went to the grocery store and a regular loaf of bread was five dollars. Five dollars! It's absolute highway robbery. 

Which, I suppose, is why I'm sitting in this chair in the first place. The bank. But no, to answer your question, I haven't lost pleasure in my trains or anything else for two weeks. Just been feeling that blankness for the last seven days.

Just completely lost my temper over a crooked tablecloth, of all things. I don't usually talk to people that way, I really don't. But he just threw it on the table all sideways. \n\nIt was one of those cheap plastic ones, you know? Not like the nice heavy linen ones my mother used to keep for Sunday dinners. She'd iron those things until they were stiff as a board, I swear. We always had roast beef on Sundays, with the carrots and the little potatoes... well, unless my father was working overtime at the mill. He was a foreman, so they called him in a lot on the weekends. Hard worker. I think that's why my knees are so bad now, I spent forty years standing on concrete just like him. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. The kid at the hall. Yeah, I just started yelling at him about the tablecloth. It wasn't right of me.\n\nDoctor: Since that incident with the tablecloth, have you found yourself losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like volunteering or other activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose you could say that. I usually do a lot of the organizing for the Día de los Muertos event down at the hall, but lately I just haven't been able to keep up with it the way I normally do. It's a shame, really. My late wife, she used to make the most incredible sugar skulls for the altars. Took her hours, mixing the sugar and the meringue powder... she had this special mold she bought on a trip to Oaxaca back in, oh, must have been '85? No, '86, because that was the year my brother bought his Ford pickup. Beautiful truck, cherry red. They don't make them like that anymore, all this computer stuff in the engines now. You can't even fix your own carburetor without a degree in electronics. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... oh, the event. Right. I just haven't been helping with it. And I've stopped going to my Thursday card nights, too. We usually play rummy, but I just don't feel steady around people right now, to be honest. I don't want to snap at one of the guys just because they take too long to discard, you know? Better to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time lately, even when you're not around other people?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'd call it a low mood, really. I just... I don't know. I sit in my chair a lot. It's one of those La-Z-Boys, you know the ones? The big plush ones. I bought it back when they still made furniture to last. It's got this nice brown corduroy fabric. My daughter hates it, says it looks like something out of the Brady Bunch, but I tell her, at least it doesn't fall apart after two years like that junk she buys from IKEA. She's always putting together some new bookshelf with a little wrench that strips the screws. I tried to help her with one last Thanksgiving, and my hands just couldn't grip the tools right anymore. Arthritis, the doctor said. Getting old isn't for the faint of heart, let me tell you. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling down. I don't think I'm sad, exactly. I just... I just sit in the chair and watch the clock a lot. I don't really feel much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you stopped enjoying things that used to bring you pleasure, like organizing events or playing cards?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, I thought I just mentioned that... maybe I didn't say it clear enough. I haven't been helping with the Día de los Muertos event, and I stopped going to my Thursday card nights. We play rummy, usually. Well, sometimes Pinochle if Frank is there, but Frank hasn't been coming around as much since his hip replacement. He went to that new hospital over on the east side, the one with the big glass front. Looks like a spaceship, if you ask me. I remember when that whole area was just empty lots and a couple of old diners. Used to get a cup of coffee for a dime at this place called Joe's. Best cherry pie in the city, too. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't been doing the events or the cards. Just... haven't felt up to it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you been feeling down or experiencing a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that again, because I was just thinking about how time seems to stretch out lately. But no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling down or sad most of the day. Like I said before, I just sit in my chair and feel... well, nothing, really. Just sort of empty or blank. \n\nIt reminds me of this one winter, back in '78 I think it was. We had that massive blizzard, snow piled up to the windowsills. We couldn't leave the house for three days. The power went out, so we had to huddle around the fireplace. My wife made a big pot of chili right before the stove died, thank goodness. We ate cold chili for two days straight. You'd think it would be awful, but it actually wasn't bad. The beans kind of soaked up all the spices... \n\nBut anyway, no, I'm not feeling sad or low, if that's what you mean. Just... not feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't think so. My mind is still pretty sharp, thankfully. I still do the crossword puzzle in the morning paper without much trouble. Well, I try to, anyway. They changed the font on the crossword a few months back, made it so tiny you practically need a microscope just to read the clues across and down. I actually called the editor of the paper to complain about it. Left a message with some secretary. Never got a call back, of course. \n\nPeople just don't have the decency to return a phone call anymore. Everything is a text message or an email now. My grandson, Tommy, he sent me a text message for my birthday last year. Just a little cartoon picture of a cake on the phone screen. I mean, you can't put a text message on the mantlepiece, can you? Used to be people sent real cards. You'd go to the pharmacy and pick out a nice Hallmark. \n\nBut, uh... no, my thinking is fine. I can concentrate just fine if I want to. I just don't have much to concentrate on these days besides the clock ticking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, didn't you just ask me that a minute ago? Or maybe it was a different question. My memory's fine, but sometimes all these questions blur together. Like I said, I wouldn't call it feeling down or depressed. I just feel kind of numb, you know? Blank. \n\nIt's sort of like how the TV screen used to look when a channel went off the air late at night. You remember that? The static? Just a bunch of black and white fuzz and that loud hissing noise. We used to have this old Zenith console television set. Weighed a ton. Had the record player built right into the top of it. I remember the day we brought it home. The delivery guys had to wedge it through the front door, scratched up the doorframe pretty good. My wife was furious. She chased them out with a broom! Oh, she had a temper, she did. But she loved that television. We used to sit and watch Ed Sullivan on it every Sunday night...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I don't feel depressed. Just... like that static on the old TV. Just a whole lot of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped finding pleasure or interest in any of the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like sitting out on the porch with my coffee and watching the blue jays. I put out peanuts for them. You have to use the unsalted kind in the shell, otherwise they won't even look at them. \n\nI learned that from my old neighbor, Frank. Frank was a real character. He had this old Ford pickup truck, a '65 I think it was, cherry red. He used to spend every single Saturday out in his driveway just waxing that thing. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps, I swear. He ended up selling it a few years back to some kid from two towns over. Made a pretty penny on it, too, but I told him he was crazy to let it go. They just don't make engines like they used to, you know? Everything is run by computers now. You open the hood of a car today and it looks like a spaceship in there. You can't even change your own spark plugs without a degree in electronics... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. No, I still like my birds and my crosswords just fine. I haven't lost interest in them or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt more tired or noticed a lack of energy lately, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm any more tired than usual. I mean, I'm 79, I'm not running marathons, but I have enough energy to get through the day. I still get up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I did when I worked at the plant. \n\nThirty-five years I worked at that manufacturing plant down by the river. We made parts for washing machines, mostly. The noise in that place, let me tell you, it was something else. Clanking and banging all day long. You had to wear these heavy ear muffs, and even then, your ears would ring for hours after your shift. I worked with a fella named Mickey. Mickey was missing half his left index finger. Lost it to a stamping press back in '78. He used to joke that it made him better at bowling, said it gave the ball a special spin. He was a funny guy, Mickey. He used to bring these elaborate sandwiches for lunch, like pastrami with coleslaw right on the bread. His wife made them. She was a lovely woman, made the best potato salad for the company picnic every year...\n\nBut, uh, energy. Yeah. No, my energy is fine. I'm not dragging myself around or anything like that. Just my normal self.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with a loss of interest in some activities and feeling empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in everything, like I told you. But this... this empty feeling, the static... it's been about a week. Ever since that robbery outside the bank. \n\nI was just going to cash my pension check, you know? They still mail the paper check. My daughter keeps telling me to set up the direct deposit on the computer, but I don't trust those things. Hackers and whatnot. You hear about it on the news all the time, people waking up and their whole life savings is just gone, poof, into thin air. I prefer to have the paper in my hand. Anyway, I always go to the same teller, Margaret. She's been there for at least fifteen years. Always asks about my granddaughter. Margaret has this little ceramic cat on her counter, right next to the pen on the chain. She said her nephew made it for her in art class... \n\nBut, yes, about a week now. Since the... the incident at the bank. Just feeling blank since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Down? Low mood? No, I wouldn't say that. Like I said, it's more of a... a blankness. A static. But not sad. I mean, I haven't been crying or moping around the house. I still keep the place tidy. You have to keep a tidy house, my late wife always said that. A cluttered house is a cluttered mind, she used to say. \n\nShe was a stickler for dusting, my Martha. Every Tuesday and Friday, out came the lemon Pledge and that yellow microfiber cloth. She wouldn't let anyone else do it, either. Said I always missed the top of the doorframes. We had this beautiful mahogany dining table, inherited it from her grandmother. Heavy thing, took four men to move it when we bought the house. She'd polish that table until it shined like a mirror. We used to host Thanksgiving every year, twenty people crammed into that dining room. The turkey always took twice as long to cook as the recipe said...\n\nBut no, I'm not down or depressed. Just... empty, I guess. And it's only been a week, not two. Ever since the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not interested in almost anything lately, even things that used to matter a lot to you?\n\nPatient: Not interested in anything? No, I wouldn't go that far. I still care about things. I still read the morning paper, front to back, every single day. The delivery boy, Tommy, he always throws it right onto the azalea bushes. I've asked him a hundred times to leave it on the porch step, but kids these days, they're always in such a rush. He rides this bright green bicycle, one of those mountain bikes with the thick tires. Reminds me of the Schwinn I bought for my son back in '85. Beautiful bike, cherry red. He rode that thing everywhere until he got his driver's license. First car he bought was a beat-up old Ford Taurus, mustard yellow. Ugliest car you've ever seen, but he loved it. Spent every weekend under the hood, trying to fix the radiator... \n\nBut anyway, no, I still care about things. I still do my crossword puzzles. It's just that... well, things just feel a bit muffled right now, if that makes sense. But I haven't lost interest in what matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Didn't I just... I thought I just answered that, Doc. No, I haven't had days like that. Like I said, it's not a low mood or being sad, just that blankness. Are you sure you're writing this down? You sound a bit like my neighbor, Arthur. He's always asking me the exact same questions twice. 'Did you get your gutters cleaned out?' 'Did you see the weather report?' Every single time I see him at the fence. \n\nHe's got this old golden retriever, Buster. Sweet dog, but completely deaf now. Arthur will stand on the back porch hollering and hollering for him, and Buster is just fast asleep under the hydrangea bushes, oblivious to the whole world. Beautiful hydrangeas, though. His wife planted those before she passed away. They turn the most vibrant shade of blue if you put coffee grounds in the soil. Martha and I tried that with our rose bushes once, but it just attracted ants. Thousands of them, marching right up the side of the brickwork... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, no. No days where I'm just sitting around sad all the time. Just the static since I was at the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two weeks in a row where you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you pulling my leg? Because I could swear you just asked me that exact same thing. Two weeks in a row, down or low mood... no, I haven't. I really haven't. It's only been a week since the whole thing at the bank anyway. \n\nYou know, this reminds me of the time I had to get my driver's license renewed down at the DMV. The clerk there—a young gal with these enormous pink fingernails, I don't know how she could even type—she asked me for my address three different times. Three times! I finally said to her, 'Miss, if I move while I'm standing in this line, I'll be sure to let you know.' The fellow behind me got a real kick out of that. He was wearing this old navy blue windbreaker, had a patch from the USS Nimitz on the shoulder. My brother served in the Navy, you know. Stationed out in San Diego back in the sixties. He used to send these postcards with the palm trees and the ocean... beautiful handwriting, cursive, the kind they don't teach in schools anymore. \n\nBut no, Doc, to answer your question again... no two weeks of being down or sad. Just the muffler feeling. The blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where most of the day, nearly every day, you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we... is there an echo in here? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. Hopeless, depressed, two weeks... I just told you, it hasn't even been two weeks since the bank robbery. It's been a week. So no, I haven't had two weeks of anything. \n\nThis is starting to feel like that movie, what was it called... Groundhog Day. With Bill Murray. Martha loved that movie. We must have rented it from Blockbuster a dozen times. Remember Blockbuster? You'd go in on a Friday night, the smell of popcorn right when you walked in the door, the blue and yellow carpet... They had this whole wall just for new releases, but you had to get there early or all the good ones were gone. I always ended up picking something from the discount bin. Found a lot of John Wayne classics that way, though. 'The Searchers,' now that's a picture. They shot it in Monument Valley. The colors of those rocks... red and orange against the blue sky. I always wanted to take an RV trip out there, drive Route 66 all the way to the coast...\n\nBut honestly, Doc, if you ask me if I've been sad for two weeks one more time, I might just have to check your hearing aid. No, no two weeks of being depressed. Just... just the blank feeling since the bank. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you do?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. Two weeks? Again with the two weeks. I keep telling you, it's only been a single week since the whole thing at the bank. So no, I haven't had two weeks of losing interest in anything, because the math just doesn't add up. \n\nBesides, I still like my hobbies. I've got an O-gauge model train setup down in the basement that takes up half the room. Started building it back in '82. Martha used to complain about the dust, but she'd come down and help me paint the little figurines anyway. We had this tiny little station master... she painted his hat a bright crimson. Reminds me of the conductor on the old Santa Fe line I took with my father when I was just a boy. The seats were covered in this terribly scratchy wool, and the smell of the diesel... boy, you don't ever forget a smell like that. My father bought me a ham sandwich in the dining car. Cost him a nickel, I think. Or maybe a dime. Things were so cheap back then. You know, yesterday I went to the grocery store and a regular loaf of bread was five dollars. Five dollars! It's absolute highway robbery. \n\nWhich, I suppose, is why I'm sitting in this chair in the first place. The bank. But no, to answer your question, I haven't lost pleasure in my trains or anything else for two weeks. Just been feeling that blankness for the last seven days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Just feeling blank since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Down? Low mood? No, I wouldn't say that. Like I said, it's more of a... a blankness. A static. But not sad. I mean, I haven't been crying or moping around the house. I still keep the place tidy. You have to keep a tidy house, my late wife always said that. A cluttered house is a cluttered mind, she used to say. \n\nShe was a stickler for dusting, my Martha. Every Tuesday and Friday, out came the lemon Pledge and that yellow microfiber cloth. She wouldn't let anyone else do it, either. Said I always missed the top of the doorframes. We had this beautiful mahogany dining table, inherited it from her grandmother. Heavy thing, took four men to move it when we bought the house. She'd polish that table until it shined like a mirror. We used to host Thanksgiving every year, twenty people crammed into that dining room. The turkey always took twice as long to cook as the recipe said...\n\nBut no, I'm not down or depressed. Just... empty, I guess. And it's only been a week, not two. Ever since the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not interested in almost anything lately, even things that used to matter a lot to you?\n\nPatient: Not interested in anything? No, I wouldn't go that far. I still care about things. I still read the morning paper, front to back, every single day. The delivery boy, Tommy, he always throws it right onto the azalea bushes. I've asked him a hundred times to leave it on the porch step, but kids these days, they're always in such a rush. He rides this bright green bicycle, one of those mountain bikes with the thick tires. Reminds me of the Schwinn I bought for my son back in '85. Beautiful bike, cherry red. He rode that thing everywhere until he got his driver's license. First car he bought was a beat-up old Ford Taurus, mustard yellow. Ugliest car you've ever seen, but he loved it. Spent every weekend under the hood, trying to fix the radiator... \n\nBut anyway, no, I still care about things. I still do my crossword puzzles. It's just that... well, things just feel a bit muffled right now, if that makes sense. But I haven't lost interest in what matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Didn't I just... I thought I just answered that, Doc. No, I haven't had days like that. Like I said, it's not a low mood or being sad, just that blankness. Are you sure you're writing this down? You sound a bit like my neighbor, Arthur. He's always asking me the exact same questions twice. 'Did you get your gutters cleaned out?' 'Did you see the weather report?' Every single time I see him at the fence. \n\nHe's got this old golden retriever, Buster. Sweet dog, but completely deaf now. Arthur will stand on the back porch hollering and hollering for him, and Buster is just fast asleep under the hydrangea bushes, oblivious to the whole world. Beautiful hydrangeas, though. His wife planted those before she passed away. They turn the most vibrant shade of blue if you put coffee grounds in the soil. Martha and I tried that with our rose bushes once, but it just attracted ants. Thousands of them, marching right up the side of the brickwork... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, no. No days where I'm just sitting around sad all the time. Just the static since I was at the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two weeks in a row where you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you pulling my leg? Because I could swear you just asked me that exact same thing. Two weeks in a row, down or low mood... no, I haven't. I really haven't. It's only been a week since the whole thing at the bank anyway. \n\nYou know, this reminds me of the time I had to get my driver's license renewed down at the DMV. The clerk there—a young gal with these enormous pink fingernails, I don't know how she could even type—she asked me for my address three different times. Three times! I finally said to her, 'Miss, if I move while I'm standing in this line, I'll be sure to let you know.' The fellow behind me got a real kick out of that. He was wearing this old navy blue windbreaker, had a patch from the USS Nimitz on the shoulder. My brother served in the Navy, you know. Stationed out in San Diego back in the sixties. He used to send these postcards with the palm trees and the ocean... beautiful handwriting, cursive, the kind they don't teach in schools anymore. \n\nBut no, Doc, to answer your question again... no two weeks of being down or sad. Just the muffler feeling. The blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where most of the day, nearly every day, you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we... is there an echo in here? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. Hopeless, depressed, two weeks... I just told you, it hasn't even been two weeks since the bank robbery. It's been a week. So no, I haven't had two weeks of anything. \n\nThis is starting to feel like that movie, what was it called... Groundhog Day. With Bill Murray. Martha loved that movie. We must have rented it from Blockbuster a dozen times. Remember Blockbuster? You'd go in on a Friday night, the smell of popcorn right when you walked in the door, the blue and yellow carpet... They had this whole wall just for new releases, but you had to get there early or all the good ones were gone. I always ended up picking something from the discount bin. Found a lot of John Wayne classics that way, though. 'The Searchers,' now that's a picture. They shot it in Monument Valley. The colors of those rocks... red and orange against the blue sky. I always wanted to take an RV trip out there, drive Route 66 all the way to the coast...\n\nBut honestly, Doc, if you ask me if I've been sad for two weeks one more time, I might just have to check your hearing aid. No, no two weeks of being depressed. Just... just the blank feeling since the bank. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you do?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. Two weeks? Again with the two weeks. I keep telling you, it's only been a single week since the whole thing at the bank. So no, I haven't had two weeks of losing interest in anything, because the math just doesn't add up. \n\nBesides, I still like my hobbies. I've got an O-gauge model train setup down in the basement that takes up half the room. Started building it back in '82. Martha used to complain about the dust, but she'd come down and help me paint the little figurines anyway. We had this tiny little station master... she painted his hat a bright crimson. Reminds me of the conductor on the old Santa Fe line I took with my father when I was just a boy. The seats were covered in this terribly scratchy wool, and the smell of the diesel... boy, you don't ever forget a smell like that. My father bought me a ham sandwich in the dining car. Cost him a nickel, I think. Or maybe a dime. Things were so cheap back then. You know, yesterday I went to the grocery store and a regular loaf of bread was five dollars. Five dollars! It's absolute highway robbery. \n\nWhich, I suppose, is why I'm sitting in this chair in the first place. The bank. Just been feeling that blankness for the last seven days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doc, I swear my ears are ringing or something. Did you just ask me about two weeks again? Two weeks of losing interest? I feel like a broken record, but no. Never in my whole life have I had two weeks where I just didn't care about anything. Not even when the factory closed down back in '98. 

That was a tough time, let me tell you. They just locked the gates on a Friday afternoon. No warning, no nothing. Three hundred men out of a job just like that. I remember walking out to the parking lot, and the sun was so bright it hurt your eyes. My buddy Sal—he drove this beat-up old Ford Pinto, the one with the hatchback that rattled every time you hit a pothole—he just sat on the hood of that car and cried. Big guy, too. Must have been two hundred and fifty pounds, used to play defensive tackle in high school. We used to go bowling every Thursday night at the lanes on 4th Street. They had the best chili dogs there. The guy who ran the grill, Hector, he'd put this secret spice blend in the chili. He told me once it was a recipe from his grandmother in Oaxaca. Never did get that recipe before he passed away...

But anyway, no. Even when the plant closed, I didn't lose interest in things for two weeks. I kept busy. Fixed up the gutters, painted the trim on the house. So no, never had two weeks of that. Just the blankness since last Tuesday at the bank.

Took her hours, mixing the sugar and the meringue powder... she had this special mold she bought on a trip to Oaxaca back in, oh, must have been '85? And I've stopped going to my Thursday card nights, too. We usually play rummy, but I just don't feel steady around people right now, to be honest. Better to just stay home.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or in a low mood most of the time lately, even when you're not around other people?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'd call it a low mood, really. It's got this nice brown corduroy fabric. My daughter hates it, says it looks like something out of the Brady Bunch, but I tell her, at least it doesn't fall apart after two years like that junk she buys from IKEA. She's always putting together some new bookshelf with a little wrench that strips the screws. I tried to help her with one last Thanksgiving, and my hands just couldn't grip the tools right anymore. Arthritis, the doctor said. Getting old isn't for the faint of heart, let me tell you. \n\nBut, uh... what were you asking? Oh, feeling down. I don't think I'm sad, exactly. I just... Well, sometimes Pinochle if Frank is there, but Frank hasn't been coming around as much since his hip replacement. He went to that new hospital over on the east side, the one with the big glass front. Looks like a spaceship, if you ask me. I remember when that whole area was just empty lots and a couple of old diners. Used to get a cup of coffee for a dime at this place called Joe's. Best cherry pie in the city, too. \n\nBut yeah, I haven't been doing the events or the cards. Just... haven't felt up to it.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you been feeling down or experiencing a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for the past couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that again, because I was just thinking about how time seems to stretch out lately. But no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling down or sad most of the day. Like I said before, I just sit in my chair and feel... well, nothing, really. Just sort of empty or blank. \n\nIt reminds me of this one winter, back in '78 I think it was. We had that massive blizzard, snow piled up to the windowsills. We couldn't leave the house for three days. The power went out, so we had to huddle around the fireplace. My wife made a big pot of chili right before the stove died, thank goodness. We ate cold chili for two days straight. You'd think it would be awful, but it actually wasn't bad. The beans kind of soaked up all the spices... \n\nBut anyway, no, I'm not feeling sad or low, if that's what you mean. Just... not feeling much of anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly or concentrate on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I don't think so. My mind is still pretty sharp, thankfully. I still do the crossword puzzle in the morning paper without much trouble. Well, I try to, anyway. They changed the font on the crossword a few months back, made it so tiny you practically need a microscope just to read the clues across and down. I actually called the editor of the paper to complain about it. Left a message with some secretary. Never got a call back, of course. \n\nPeople just don't have the decency to return a phone call anymore. Everything is a text message or an email now. My grandson, Tommy, he sent me a text message for my birthday last year. Just a little cartoon picture of a cake on the phone screen. I mean, you can't put a text message on the mantlepiece, can you? Used to be people sent real cards. You'd go to the pharmacy and pick out a nice Hallmark. \n\nBut, uh... no, my thinking is fine. I can concentrate just fine if I want to. I just don't have much to concentrate on these days besides the clock ticking.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, didn't you just ask me that a minute ago? Or maybe it was a different question. My memory's fine, but sometimes all these questions blur together. Like I said, I wouldn't call it feeling down or depressed. I just feel kind of numb, you know? Blank. \n\nIt's sort of like how the TV screen used to look when a channel went off the air late at night. You remember that? The static? Just a bunch of black and white fuzz and that loud hissing noise. We used to have this old Zenith console television set. Weighed a ton. Had the record player built right into the top of it. I remember the day we brought it home. The delivery guys had to wedge it through the front door, scratched up the doorframe pretty good. My wife was furious. She chased them out with a broom! Oh, she had a temper, she did. But she loved that television. We used to sit and watch Ed Sullivan on it every Sunday night...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right. No, I don't feel depressed. Just... like that static on the old TV. Just a whole lot of nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped finding pleasure or interest in any of the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Completely stopped? No, I wouldn't say that. I still like sitting out on the porch with my coffee and watching the blue jays. I put out peanuts for them. You have to use the unsalted kind in the shell, otherwise they won't even look at them. \n\nI learned that from my old neighbor, Frank. Frank was a real character. He had this old Ford pickup truck, a '65 I think it was, cherry red. He used to spend every single Saturday out in his driveway just waxing that thing. You could see your reflection in the hubcaps, I swear. He ended up selling it a few years back to some kid from two towns over. Made a pretty penny on it, too, but I told him he was crazy to let it go. They just don't make engines like they used to, you know? Everything is run by computers now. You open the hood of a car today and it looks like a spaceship in there. You can't even change your own spark plugs without a degree in electronics... \n\nBut, uh, what was the question? Oh, enjoying things. No, I still like my birds and my crosswords just fine. I haven't lost interest in them or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt more tired or noticed a lack of energy lately, even when you’re not doing much?\n\nPatient: Tired? No, I wouldn't say I'm any more tired than usual. I mean, I'm 79, I'm not running marathons, but I have enough energy to get through the day. I still get up at 6:00 AM sharp, same as I did when I worked at the plant. \n\nThirty-five years I worked at that manufacturing plant down by the river. We made parts for washing machines, mostly. The noise in that place, let me tell you, it was something else. Clanking and banging all day long. You had to wear these heavy ear muffs, and even then, your ears would ring for hours after your shift. I worked with a fella named Mickey. Mickey was missing half his left index finger. Lost it to a stamping press back in '78. He used to joke that it made him better at bowling, said it gave the ball a special spin. He was a funny guy, Mickey. He used to bring these elaborate sandwiches for lunch, like pastrami with coleslaw right on the bread. His wife made them. She was a lovely woman, made the best potato salad for the company picnic every year...\n\nBut, uh, energy. Yeah. No, my energy is fine. I'm not dragging myself around or anything like that. Just my normal self.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with a loss of interest in some activities and feeling empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in everything, like I told you. But this... this empty feeling, the static... it's been about a week. Ever since that robbery outside the bank. \n\nI was just going to cash my pension check, you know? They still mail the paper check. My daughter keeps telling me to set up the direct deposit on the computer, but I don't trust those things. Hackers and whatnot. You hear about it on the news all the time, people waking up and their whole life savings is just gone, poof, into thin air. I prefer to have the paper in my hand. Anyway, I always go to the same teller, Margaret. She's been there for at least fifteen years. Always asks about my granddaughter. Margaret has this little ceramic cat on her counter, right next to the pen on the chain. She said her nephew made it for her in art class... \n\nBut, yes, about a week now. Since the... the incident at the bank. Just feeling blank since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: Down? Low mood? No, I wouldn't say that. Like I said, it's more of a... a blankness. A static. But not sad. I mean, I haven't been crying or moping around the house. I still keep the place tidy. You have to keep a tidy house, my late wife always said that. A cluttered house is a cluttered mind, she used to say. \n\nShe was a stickler for dusting, my Martha. Every Tuesday and Friday, out came the lemon Pledge and that yellow microfiber cloth. She wouldn't let anyone else do it, either. Said I always missed the top of the doorframes. We had this beautiful mahogany dining table, inherited it from her grandmother. Heavy thing, took four men to move it when we bought the house. She'd polish that table until it shined like a mirror. We used to host Thanksgiving every year, twenty people crammed into that dining room. The turkey always took twice as long to cook as the recipe said...\n\nBut no, I'm not down or depressed. Just... empty, I guess. And it's only been a week, not two. Ever since the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not interested in almost anything lately, even things that used to matter a lot to you?\n\nPatient: Not interested in anything? No, I wouldn't go that far. I still care about things. I still read the morning paper, front to back, every single day. The delivery boy, Tommy, he always throws it right onto the azalea bushes. I've asked him a hundred times to leave it on the porch step, but kids these days, they're always in such a rush. He rides this bright green bicycle, one of those mountain bikes with the thick tires. Reminds me of the Schwinn I bought for my son back in '85. Beautiful bike, cherry red. He rode that thing everywhere until he got his driver's license. First car he bought was a beat-up old Ford Taurus, mustard yellow. Ugliest car you've ever seen, but he loved it. Spent every weekend under the hood, trying to fix the radiator... \n\nBut anyway, no, I still care about things. I still do my crossword puzzles. It's just that... well, things just feel a bit muffled right now, if that makes sense. But I haven't lost interest in what matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Didn't I just... I thought I just answered that, Doc. No, I haven't had days like that. Like I said, it's not a low mood or being sad, just that blankness. Are you sure you're writing this down? You sound a bit like my neighbor, Arthur. He's always asking me the exact same questions twice. 'Did you get your gutters cleaned out?' 'Did you see the weather report?' Every single time I see him at the fence. \n\nHe's got this old golden retriever, Buster. Sweet dog, but completely deaf now. Arthur will stand on the back porch hollering and hollering for him, and Buster is just fast asleep under the hydrangea bushes, oblivious to the whole world. Beautiful hydrangeas, though. His wife planted those before she passed away. They turn the most vibrant shade of blue if you put coffee grounds in the soil. Martha and I tried that with our rose bushes once, but it just attracted ants. Thousands of them, marching right up the side of the brickwork... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, no. No days where I'm just sitting around sad all the time. Just the static since I was at the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two weeks in a row where you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you pulling my leg? Because I could swear you just asked me that exact same thing. Two weeks in a row, down or low mood... no, I haven't. I really haven't. It's only been a week since the whole thing at the bank anyway. \n\nYou know, this reminds me of the time I had to get my driver's license renewed down at the DMV. The clerk there—a young gal with these enormous pink fingernails, I don't know how she could even type—she asked me for my address three different times. Three times! I finally said to her, 'Miss, if I move while I'm standing in this line, I'll be sure to let you know.' The fellow behind me got a real kick out of that. He was wearing this old navy blue windbreaker, had a patch from the USS Nimitz on the shoulder. My brother served in the Navy, you know. Stationed out in San Diego back in the sixties. He used to send these postcards with the palm trees and the ocean... beautiful handwriting, cursive, the kind they don't teach in schools anymore. \n\nBut no, Doc, to answer your question again... no two weeks of being down or sad. Just the muffler feeling. The blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where most of the day, nearly every day, you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we... is there an echo in here? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. Hopeless, depressed, two weeks... I just told you, it hasn't even been two weeks since the bank robbery. It's been a week. So no, I haven't had two weeks of anything. \n\nThis is starting to feel like that movie, what was it called... Groundhog Day. With Bill Murray. Martha loved that movie. We must have rented it from Blockbuster a dozen times. Remember Blockbuster? You'd go in on a Friday night, the smell of popcorn right when you walked in the door, the blue and yellow carpet... They had this whole wall just for new releases, but you had to get there early or all the good ones were gone. I always ended up picking something from the discount bin. Found a lot of John Wayne classics that way, though. 'The Searchers,' now that's a picture. They shot it in Monument Valley. The colors of those rocks... red and orange against the blue sky. I always wanted to take an RV trip out there, drive Route 66 all the way to the coast...\n\nBut honestly, Doc, if you ask me if I've been sad for two weeks one more time, I might just have to check your hearing aid. No, no two weeks of being depressed. Just... just the blank feeling since the bank. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you do?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. Two weeks? Again with the two weeks. I keep telling you, it's only been a single week since the whole thing at the bank. So no, I haven't had two weeks of losing interest in anything, because the math just doesn't add up. \n\nBesides, I still like my hobbies. I've got an O-gauge model train setup down in the basement that takes up half the room. Started building it back in '82. Martha used to complain about the dust, but she'd come down and help me paint the little figurines anyway. We had this tiny little station master... she painted his hat a bright crimson. Reminds me of the conductor on the old Santa Fe line I took with my father when I was just a boy. The seats were covered in this terribly scratchy wool, and the smell of the diesel... boy, you don't ever forget a smell like that. My father bought me a ham sandwich in the dining car. Cost him a nickel, I think. Or maybe a dime. Things were so cheap back then. You know, yesterday I went to the grocery store and a regular loaf of bread was five dollars. Five dollars! It's absolute highway robbery. \n\nWhich, I suppose, is why I'm sitting in this chair in the first place. The bank. But no, to answer your question, I haven't lost pleasure in my trains or anything else for two weeks. Just been feeling that blankness for the last seven days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Doc, I swear my ears are ringing or something. Did you just ask me about two weeks again? Two weeks of losing interest? I feel like a broken record, but no. Never in my whole life have I had two weeks where I just didn't care about anything. Not even when the factory closed down back in '98. \n\nThat was a tough time, let me tell you. They just locked the gates on a Friday afternoon. No warning, no nothing. Three hundred men out of a job just like that. I remember walking out to the parking lot, and the sun was so bright it hurt your eyes. My buddy Sal—he drove this beat-up old Ford Pinto, the one with the hatchback that rattled every time you hit a pothole—he just sat on the hood of that car and cried. Big guy, too. Must have been two hundred and fifty pounds, used to play defensive tackle in high school. We used to go bowling every Thursday night at the lanes on 4th Street. They had the best chili dogs there. The guy who ran the grill, Hector, he'd put this secret spice blend in the chili. He told me once it was a recipe from his grandmother in Oaxaca. Never did get that recipe before he passed away...\n\nBut anyway, no. Even when the plant closed, I didn't lose interest in things for two weeks. I kept busy. Fixed up the gutters, painted the trim on the house. So no, never had two weeks of that. Just the blankness since last Tuesday at the bank.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's just that... well, things just feel a bit muffled right now, if that makes sense. But I haven't lost interest in what matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or in a low mood most of the time, not just a little sad here and there?\n\nPatient: Didn't I just... I thought I just answered that, Doc. No, I haven't had days like that. Like I said, it's not a low mood or being sad, just that blankness. Are you sure you're writing this down? You sound a bit like my neighbor, Arthur. He's always asking me the exact same questions twice. 'Did you get your gutters cleaned out?' 'Did you see the weather report?' Every single time I see him at the fence. \n\nHe's got this old golden retriever, Buster. Sweet dog, but completely deaf now. Arthur will stand on the back porch hollering and hollering for him, and Buster is just fast asleep under the hydrangea bushes, oblivious to the whole world. Beautiful hydrangeas, though. His wife planted those before she passed away. They turn the most vibrant shade of blue if you put coffee grounds in the soil. Martha and I tried that with our rose bushes once, but it just attracted ants. Thousands of them, marching right up the side of the brickwork... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Right, no. No days where I'm just sitting around sad all the time. Just the static since I was at the bank.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any two weeks in a row where you felt down or in a low mood most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you pulling my leg? Because I could swear you just asked me that exact same thing. Two weeks in a row, down or low mood... no, I haven't. I really haven't. It's only been a week since the whole thing at the bank anyway. \n\nYou know, this reminds me of the time I had to get my driver's license renewed down at the DMV. The clerk there—a young gal with these enormous pink fingernails, I don't know how she could even type—she asked me for my address three different times. Three times! I finally said to her, 'Miss, if I move while I'm standing in this line, I'll be sure to let you know.' The fellow behind me got a real kick out of that. He was wearing this old navy blue windbreaker, had a patch from the USS Nimitz on the shoulder. My brother served in the Navy, you know. Stationed out in San Diego back in the sixties. He used to send these postcards with the palm trees and the ocean... beautiful handwriting, cursive, the kind they don't teach in schools anymore. \n\nBut no, Doc, to answer your question again... no two weeks of being down or sad. Just the muffler feeling. The blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where most of the day, nearly every day, you felt down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Doc, are we... is there an echo in here? I feel like I'm stuck in a loop. Hopeless, depressed, two weeks... I just told you, it hasn't even been two weeks since the bank robbery. It's been a week. So no, I haven't had two weeks of anything. \n\nThis is starting to feel like that movie, what was it called... Groundhog Day. With Bill Murray. Martha loved that movie. We must have rented it from Blockbuster a dozen times. Remember Blockbuster? You'd go in on a Friday night, the smell of popcorn right when you walked in the door, the blue and yellow carpet... They had this whole wall just for new releases, but you had to get there early or all the good ones were gone. I always ended up picking something from the discount bin. Found a lot of John Wayne classics that way, though. 'The Searchers,' now that's a picture. They shot it in Monument Valley. The colors of those rocks... red and orange against the blue sky. I always wanted to take an RV trip out there, drive Route 66 all the way to the coast...\n\nBut honestly, Doc, if you ask me if I've been sad for two weeks one more time, I might just have to check your hearing aid. No, no two weeks of being depressed. Just... just the blank feeling since the bank. That's all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least two weeks where you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything you do?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall here. Two weeks? Again with the two weeks. I keep telling you, it's only been a single week since the whole thing at the bank. So no, I haven't had two weeks of losing interest in anything, because the math just doesn't add up. \n\nBesides, I still like my hobbies. I've got an O-gauge model train setup down in the basement that takes up half the room. Started building it back in '82. Martha used to complain about the dust, but she'd come down and help me paint the little figurines anyway. We had this tiny little station master... she painted his hat a bright crimson. Reminds me of the conductor on the old Santa Fe line I took with my father when I was just a boy. The seats were covered in this terribly scratchy wool, and the smell of the diesel... boy, you don't ever forget a smell like that. My father bought me a ham sandwich in the dining car. Cost him a nickel, I think. Or maybe a dime. Things were so cheap back then. You know, yesterday I went to the grocery store and a regular loaf of bread was five dollars. Five dollars! It's absolute highway robbery. \n\nWhich, I suppose, is why I'm sitting in this chair in the first place. The bank. But no, to answer your question, I haven't lost pleasure in my trains or anything else for two weeks. Just been feeling that blankness for the last seven days.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you lost interest or pleasure in almost everything for two weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Doc, I swear my ears are ringing or something. Did you just ask me about two weeks again? Two weeks of losing interest? I feel like a broken record, but no. Never in my whole life have I had two weeks where I just didn't care about anything. Not even when the factory closed down back in '98. \n\nThat was a tough time, let me tell you. They just locked the gates on a Friday afternoon. No warning, no nothing. Three hundred men out of a job just like that. I remember walking out to the parking lot, and the sun was so bright it hurt your eyes. My buddy Sal—he drove this beat-up old Ford Pinto, the one with the hatchback that rattled every time you hit a pothole—he just sat on the hood of that car and cried. Big guy, too. Must have been two hundred and fifty pounds, used to play defensive tackle in high school. We used to go bowling every Thursday night at the lanes on 4th Street. They had the best chili dogs there. The guy who ran the grill, Hector, he'd put this secret spice blend in the chili. He told me once it was a recipe from his grandmother in Oaxaca. Never did get that recipe before he passed away...\n\nBut anyway, no. Even when the plant closed, I didn't lose interest in things for two weeks. I kept busy. Fixed up the gutters, painted the trim on the house. So no, never had two weeks of that. Just the blankness since last Tuesday at the bank.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doc, I... I'm starting to think one of us is having a senior moment, and for once, I don't think it's me. You're asking about two weeks of being depressed again? But in the past this time? No, no, I've never had a time like that. Never. 

I've always been a pretty steady guy. Even when things got rough, like when Martha got sick. That was... well, that was hard. But I didn't get depressed for weeks on end. I had to take care of her, you know? Make sure she had her tea just the way she liked it—Earl Grey, steep it for exactly three minutes, not a second longer, with just a splash of milk. She was very particular about the milk. Said if you put it in first, it ruined the whole cup. I read somewhere once that the Queen does it the exact opposite way, puts the milk in first to stop the fine china from cracking. Course, we didn't have fine china. We had these thick ceramic mugs we got at a craft fair in Vermont back in '76. The bicentennial year. Everything was painted red, white, and blue that year. I bought a commemorative coin that I still have in a drawer somewhere...

Anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I've never been depressed or hopeless for two weeks straight in my whole life. Just dealing with this... this weird blank feeling since the bank robbery last week. That's the only thing.
